PURPOSE AND OVERVIEW OF REBUTTAL 
TESTIMONY 
Q.
What is the purpose of your Rebuttal Testimony? 
15 
A.
The purpose of our Rebuttal Testimony is to respond to the Prepared 
16 
Testimony of the Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) Finance 
17 
Panel regarding the appropriate return on equity (“ROE”) for Corning 
18 
Natural Gas Corporation (“Corning Gas”).  Our analysis is supported by the 
19 
data presented in Exhibits__ (AEB/CMW-1R) through (AEB/CMW-9R). 
20 


--- Page 4 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
2 
Q. 
Please summarize your principal concerns with the Staff Finance 
3 
Panel's recommendations. 
4 
A. 
The Staff Finance Panel's recommended ROE of 8.45 percent is 55-basis 
5 
points lower than the current authorized ROE for Corning Gas of 9.00 
6 
percent (2017). Staff's recommendation is also well below any authorized 
7 
ROE for a natural gas distribution company in the U.S. since at least 1980.1
8 
Staff has provided no evidence to demonstrate that the Company's risk 
9 
profiles is sufficiently different from other natural gas distribution 
10 
companies to justify a return that is 116-basis points lower than the average 
11 
ROE award for other gas distribution companies over the period from 2018 
12 
through 2020. As shown in Figure 1, Staff's ROE recommendation of 8.45 
13 
percent is well below the lowest authorized ROE for a gas distributor during 
14 
this time period. 
15 
1 
Source: S&P Global Market Intelligence (Formerly known as SNL Financial).

Authorized ROE 
Staff applied a two-thirds weighting to the results of the Discounted Cash 
Flow ("DCF") analysis and a one-third weight to the Capital Asset Pricing 
Model ("CAPM") analysis, based on the specifications of the 1991 
Recommended Decision ("RD") in the Generic Finance Proceeding 
("GFP") and other Commission discussions of the ROE estimation 
methodology. Staff's entire justification for its methodology is that the 
Commission has used this approach in prior cases. While Staff cites to other 
cases, it fails to recognize that the Commission has made adjustments to its 
methodology since the RD, to rely on better and more current information. 
Staff themselves have suggested adjustments to the Commission's 
methodology in prior cases in response to disruptions in financial markets. 
The Commission's methodology has evolved over time, suggesting that the 
Commission is open to considering whether the data and methodologies that

1 
it has historically relied upon are the best and most relevant given prevailing 
2 
market conditions and financial analysis best practices at the time of the 
3 
case it is deciding. 
4 
5 
In contrast to the Commission's demonstrated willingness to change its 
6 
approach to reflect the circumstances at the time of its decision, Staff has 
7 
not questioned why the result of its ROE analyses is inconsistent with 
8 
authorized returns available in New York and other jurisdictions, and 
9 
whether that difference is, or can be, accounted for by evidence. Rather, 
10 
Staff has applied the models in a purely mechanistic fashion based on 
11 
Commission "precedent" without using informed judgment or considering 
12 
current economic and market conditions, as is appropriate and as the 
13 
Commission has demonstrated a willingness to do in prior cases. 
14 
15

16 
Consolidated Edison Company of New York ("CECONY") that included 
17 
an authorized ROE of 8.80 percent and an equity ratio of 48.00 percent.2 As 
18 
described in our Supplemental Direct Testimony, Moody's subsequently 
19 
downgraded CECONY's credit rating from A3 to Baal, citing expected 
20 
weaker financial metrics following the rate case order.3 Since the issuance 
21 
of that order, capital markets have experienced levels of volatility not seen

1 
since the height of the Great Recession in 2008/09 due to the economic 
2 
effects of COVID-19. Despite these factors, and even though Corning Gas 
3 
is just a small fraction of the size of CECONY, Staff recommends an ROE 
4 
and an equity ratio for Corning Gas that are both lower than the Commission 
5 
authorized for CECONY. 
6 
7 
Staff has not considered how debt or equity investors would react to an 
8 
extraordinarily low authorized ROE for the Company relative to recently 
9 
approved ROEs. Such a low ROE is likely to be viewed as punitive and 
10 
cause investors to question the level of regulatory support being provided 
11 
by the Commission, with consequences that could be borne by all New York 
12 
utilities and their customers. Credit rating agencies have already observed 
13 
that the operating environment for gas utilities in New York has become 
14 
"more challenging" due to (1) lower than average authorized ROEs and 
15 
equity ratios, (2) the economic effects of COVID-19, and (3) the increasing 
16 
politicization of gas utility operations.4 It is not clear how Staff determined 
17 
that an ROE that is the lowest authorized ROE since at least 1980 for a 
18 
natural gas distribution utility would be viewed by the investment 
19 
community as predictable. Furthermore, it is not likely that investors would 
20 
view Staff's recommended ROE for Corning Gas as predictable since it is 
21 
even 35 to 55 basis points below the ROEs authorized for electric and 
22 
natural gas distribution companies in New York from 2018 to the beginning

7 
8 
The GFP was established in 1991 to address the sensitivity of the DCF 
9 
model to interest rate fluctuations and to address the unreasonably low 
10 
returns that are produced by this methodology when interest rates are low. 
11 
The interest rate environment today is certainly much lower than in 1991 
12 
when the Commission decided that the DCF model was not producing 
13 
reasonable results. While the RD specified conventions for the DCF and 
14 
CAPM in the GFP, the RD in that case left open the opportunity to consider 
15 
other methods for "good reason".7
16 
17 
The results of Staffs DCF model in this case provide that good reason. At 
18 
a time when market conditions have been extraordinarily volatile and utility 
19 
stocks have traded more like the overall market and less like a "safe haven"

1 
investment, Staff's application of the DCF model results in an ROE estimate 
2 
of 8.43 percent, and its CAPM results in an ROE estimate of 8.55 percent. 
3 
These estimates are both lower than the lowest ROE authorized by the 
4 
Commission in the past 35 years and 106 to 118-basis points lower than the 
5 
average authorized ROE for gas distribution companies in all U.S. 
6 
jurisdictions since January 2018. The assumptions and inputs used to 
7 
develop Staff's DCF and CAPM models do not result in reasonable 
8 
estimates of the expected ROE. Benchmarking Staffs DCF and CAPM 
9 
results against other observable authorized ROEs supports the position that 
10 
the parameters of the ROE estimation models in this proceeding must be 
11 
modified to reflect current market conditions. Our Rebuttal Testimony 
12 
demonstrates that reasonable adjustments to the models result in returns, 
13 
using Staff's methodology, and the equal weighting proposed in our Direct 
14 
Testimony, that are reasonable and consistent with the returns provided on 
15 
investments of comparable risk. 
16 
17 
The GFP provided a framework for the Commission to evaluate whether the 
18 
fmancial models used to estimate an appropriate ROE were producing 
19 
reasonable results under a given set of market conditions. The Commission 
20 
has since considered its methodology and has made modifications to its 
21 
preferred approach and assumptions to more reasonably reflect market 
22 
conditions. 
Specifically, the Commission has modified the averaging 
23 
periods relied on for stock prices in the DCF model and the bond yields used

in the CAPM from six months to three months to more appropriately reflect 
market data. In addition, the Commission has changed how it estimates the 
market risk premium from relying on historical data to projected data, 
abandoning stale data in favor of data that more accurately reflects market 
conditions that are relevant in setting the ROE. 
Even Staff has 
recommended adjustments to the ROE estimation methodology based on a 
divergence between the mean and median DCF results produced by its 
model.' Based on recent market conditions, it would be reasonable for the 
Commission to again evaluate the relevant market data points to be used in 
the ROE estimation methodologies to best estimate the forward-looking 
cost of equity. For the same reasons, it would be reasonable for the 
Commission to reconsider the weighting placed on the DCF and CAPM 
13 
approaches to establish a forward-looking ROE that meets the 
14 
comparability standards of Hope and Bluefield.

21 
in and out of various asset classes responding to both

positive and negative developments regarding the COVID-

Q. 
How do you respond? 
9 
A.

10 
recovered from the effects of the coronavirus pandemic, the volatility Staff 
11 
notes would still affect its model results. Staff's DCF and CAPM analyses 
12 
reflect market data from March, April, and May of 2020.14 That is precisely 
13 
the period in which Staff admits financial markets were "quite erratic".

20 
stocks "rose sharply" in the most recent Value Line reports.15 However,

Q. 
What steps have the Federal Reserve and the U.S. Congress taken to 
5 
stabilize financial markets and support the economy? 
6 
A. 
On March 23, 2020, the Federal Reserve began expansive programs to 
7 
support credit to large employers; the Primary Market Corporate Credit 
8 
Facility (PMCCF) to provide liquidity for new issuances of corporate 
9 
bonds, and the Secondary Market Corporate Credit Facility (SMCCF) to 
10 
provide liquidity for outstanding corporate debt issuances. Further, the 
11 
Federal Reserve supported the flow of credit to consumers and businesses 
12 
through the Term Asset-Backed Securities Loan Facility (TALF).21
20

updated data in Figure 3.

measures to support the economy", March 23, 2020.

Additionally, on March 27, 2020, the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and

Q. 
Are there indicators that measure the uncertainty in the global 
9 
economy related to COVID-19? 
10 
A. 
Yes. Similar to the U.S. trade policy uncertainty index that we reviewed in 
11 
our Direct Testimony,' economists Scott Baker, Nicholas Bloom and 
12 
Steven Davis have also developed a global economic policy uncertainty 
13 
index. This index is a GDP-weighted average of the economic policy 
14 
uncertainty index of 21 countries. The economic policy uncertainty index 
15 
measures the frequency that articles in publications of a country discuss 
16 
economic policy uncertainty. Figure 4 shows that uncertainty regarding 
17 
global economic policy is at its highest level since at least 1997, with the 
18 
largest increase occurring in the last two years as a result of the trade dispute 
19 
between the U.S. and China and the spread of COVID-19. 
20 
22

uncertainty index of 21 countries. The economic policy uncertainty index 
14 
measures the frequency that articles in publications of a country discuss 
15 
economic policy uncertainty.   Figure 4 shows that uncertainty regarding 
16 
global economic policy is at its highest level since at least 1997, with the 
17 
largest increase occurring in the last two years as a result of the trade dispute 
18 
between the U.S. and China and the spread of COVID-19.

1 
Q. 
Do you agree with Staff that because the market has "mostly 
2 
recovered" from the lows in March it is reasonable to conclude that the 
3 
ROE models "appropriately capture" recent market conditions?" 
4 
A. 
No. As noted above, the recovery in equity markets was mainly driven by 
5 
the unprecedented intervention of the Federal Reserve and Congress which 
6 
provided short-term stability to the debt and equity markets. In addition, 
7 
there have been significant periods of volatility that have extended beyond 
8 
March. Finally, as discussed previously, based on the production schedule 
9 
adhered to by Value Line, the data relied upon in Staff's models does not 
10 
appropriately capture market conditions. 
Eleven of Staff's proxy 
11 
companies have not been updated by Value Line to reflect the change in 
12 
market conditions. Therefore, it is incorrect to assume like Staff has that 
13 
the economic effects of COVID-19 are currently reflected in its ROE 
14 
results. 
15 
16 
Q.

17 
conditions on regulated utilities? 
18 
A. 
Yes. In April 2020, Standard & Poor's (S&P) downgraded the outlook on 
19 
the entire North American utilities sector indicating that 25 percent of the 
20 
industry was previously on a negative outlook or CreditWatch with negative 
21 
implications and that S&P expected that COVID-19 would create

economic effects of COVID-19 have created an unprecedented credit shock

10

1 
[h]owever, amid the drop in stocks in February and 
2 
March, the historically low-equity-beta Utilities sector 
3 
simply didn't play its traditional relative safe-haven role. 
4 
The sharp drop in interest rates would normally be 
5 
expected to provide relative support to this sector, which 
6 
relies on high levels of debt and tends to pay relatively 
7 
high dividends—often an attraction for investors when 
8 
yields on fixed income investments are low. However, 
9 
there were unique circumstances that outweighed these 
10 
historical relationships. 
11 
12 
For one thing, because some investors had already been 
13 
reaching for yield before the crisis began, the high-
14 
dividend-paying Utilities sector had been bid up to 
15 
record-high valuation levels. Even underperformance 
16 
year-to-date hasn't fully reversed those relatively high 
17 
valuations, so we're not confident the sector will return 
18 
to its defensive roots if markets sell off again. 
19 
20 
Additionally, with improving prospects for economic 
21 
growth, longer-term yields have begun to nudge higher, 
22 
which further reduces the sector's attractiveness. The 
23 
fundamentals of the sector remain relatively strong, 
24 
owing to still-low interest rates supporting capital 
25 
expenditure needs and stability of earnings growth 
26 
expectations. However, the sector's short-term relative 
27 
performance has lagged amid a turn towards more 
28 
cyclical sectors. Therefore, we are underweighting 
29 
Utilities, despite expected bouts of volatility in the 
30 
overall market.32

4 
expected to provide relative support to this sector, which 
5 
relies on high levels of debt and tends to pay relatively 
6 
high dividends—often an attraction for investors when 
7 
yields on fixed income investments are low. However, 
8 
there were unique circumstances that outweighed these 
9 
historical relationships.  
10 
11 
For one thing, because some investors had already been 
12 
reaching for yield before the crisis began, the high-
13 
dividend-paying Utilities sector had been bid up to 
14 
record-high valuation levels. Even underperformance 
15 
year-to-date hasn’t fully reversed those relatively high 
16 
valuations, so we're not confident the sector will return 
17 
to its defensive roots if markets sell off again.  
18 
19 
Additionally, with improving prospects for economic 
20 
growth, longer-term yields have begun to nudge higher, 
21 
which further reduces the sector's attractiveness. The 
22 
fundamentals of the sector remain relatively strong, 
23 
owing to still-low interest rates supporting capital 
24 
expenditure needs and stability of earnings growth 
25 
expectations. However, the sector's short-term relative 
26 
performance has lagged amid a turn towards more 
27 
cyclical sectors. Therefore, we are underweighting 
28 
Utilities, despite expected bouts of volatility in the 
29 
overall market.32
30 
31

34

35 
prices that occurred as a result of the economic effects of COVID-19.  For 
36 
example, Barron’s recently noted:

Q. 
How did Staff adjust its model in response to market disruptions in 16-
8 
G-0369? 
9 
A. 
Staff states that, prior to 2016, it consistently relied on the median result of 
10 
its DCF model in order to mitigate the influence of outlier results.35
11 
However, in 2016, Staff observed that the mean and median results of its 
12 
DCF model began diverging. In response to this divergence, Staff began 
13 
utilizing the mean, rather than the median, result of its DCF analysis. 
14 
Indeed, Staff relied on the mean result of its DCF analysis in this case.36
15 
16 
Generally, the mean result of an analysis is more susceptible to outlier data 
17 
points than the median result because each individual result is given equal 
18 
weight. Divergence between the mean and median results indicates the 
19 
potential presence of outlier data points. 
Therefore, while Staff has 
20 
recognized the divergence between the mean and median results of the DCF 
21 
model indicates an underlying issue with the results of the DCF model, Staff

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
While the policies of the Federal Reserve have stabilized the corporate bond 
2 
market, the result has been an increase in equity prices, as investors have 
3 
had to move along the risk spectrum in search of returns. Staff has only 
4 
highlighted the recent decline in measures of volatility relative to their peaks 
5 
in March, however, financial markets have by no means "recovered" from 
6 
the effects of COVID-19 and much uncertainty still remains. 
7 
8 
ROE estimation models that rely on recent market data must be interpreted 
9 
with extreme caution. 
For example, the DCF model relies on the share 
10 
prices for the proxy companies, which have been extremely volatile in the 
11 
last several months and are likely currently influenced by the policies of the 
12 
Federal Reserve; these prices are not likely representative of what should 
13 
be expected during the period that Corning Gas' rates will be in effect. 
14 
15 
As a result, there are two key factors that must be considered when 
16 
determining the ROE for Corning Gas: 1) current and prospective market 
17 
conditions should be considered when determining where, within the range 
18 
of results, Corning Gas' authorized ROE should be set, and 2) where 
19 
possible, it is necessary to consider projected market data in each of the 
20 
models, which reflect economists' expectations for the market conditions 
21 
that will prevail during the period that Corning Gas' rates will be in effect.

Q. 
What is Staff's ROE recommendation in this proceeding? 
2 
A. 
Staff recommends an ROE of 8.45 percent, based on a two-thirds weighting 
3 
of its DCF model results of 8.43 percent and a one-third weighting of its 
4 
average CAPM results of 8.55 percent.38
5 
6 
Q. 
What are the primary areas of disagreement between Staff and you as 
7 
it relates to the authorized ROE for the Company? 
8 
A. 
The primary area of disagreement between our analysis and 
9 
recommendation and that of the Staff is that Staff has dogmatically 
10 
developed the assumptions used in their ROE formula without considering 
11 
the effect of market conditions on the models, or the current financial profile 
12 
of the Company. As a result, Staff simply runs the models and recommends 
13 
the results that the model generates. 
As discussed in our Rebuttal 
14 
Testimony, Staff's model results and ultimate recommendations are not 
15 
consistent with recently authorized returns in any other jurisdiction.

18 
based on the principle of consistency with prior Commission

methodologies. Staff refers multiple times to Commission precedent as the 
20 
basis for key assumptions in its ROE estimation methodology.

foundation of Staff's criticisms of our methodology is simply that the

PROXY GROUP COMPOSITION

10

14

--- Page 36 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
That proxy group arrangements must be risk-
2 
appropriate is the common theme in each argument. 
3 
The principle is well-established. See Hope Natural Gas 
4 
Co., 320 U.S. at 603 ("[T]he return to the equity owner 
5 
should be commensurate with returns on investments in 
6 
other enterprises having corresponding risks."); CAPP 
7 
I, 254 F.3d at 293 ("[A] utility must offer a risk-adjusted 
8 
expected rate of return sufficient to attract investors."). 
9 
The principle captures what proxy groups do, namely, 
10 
provide market-determined stock and dividend figures 
11 
from public companies comparable to a target company 
12 
for which those figures are unavailable. CAPP I, 254 
13 
F.3d at 293-94. 
Market-determined stock figures 
14 
reflect a company's risk level and, when combined with 
15 
dividend values, permit calculation of the "risk-adjusted 
16 
expected rate of return sufficient to attract investors." 
17 
*** 
18 
What matters is that the overall proxy group 
19 
arrangement makes sense in terms of relative risk and, 
20 
even more importantly, in terms of the statutory 
21 
command to set "just and reasonable" rates, 15 U.S.C. § 
22 
717c, that are "commensurate with returns on 
23 
investments in other enterprises having corresponding 
24 
risks" and "sufficient to assure confidence in the 
25 
fmancial integrity of the enterprise . . . [and] maintain 
26 
its credit and . . . attract capital," Hope Natural Gas Co., 
27 
320 U.S. at 603.° 
28 
29 
Consistent with the DC Circuit's decision, we have selected a proxy group 
30 
of companies with comparable investment risk to Corning Gas. In contrast, 
31 
Staff applied less stringent screening criteria, which result in a larger, but 
32 
less comparable proxy group. 
33 
40 
Petal Gas Storage v. FERC, 496 F.3d 695, 699-700 (D.C. Cir. 2007).

reflect a company’s risk level and, when combined with 
14 
dividend values, permit calculation of the “risk-adjusted 
15 
expected rate of return sufficient to attract investors.” 
16 
*** 
17 
What matters is that the overall proxy group 
18 
arrangement makes sense in terms of relative risk and, 
19 
even more importantly, in terms of the statutory 
20 
command to set “just and reasonable” rates, 15 U.S.C. § 
21 
717c, that are “commensurate with returns on 
22 
investments in other enterprises having corresponding 
23 
risks” and “sufficient to assure confidence in the 
24 
financial integrity of the enterprise . . . [and] maintain 
25 
its credit and . . . attract capital,” Hope Natural Gas Co., 
26 
320 U.S. at 603.40
27 
28 
Consistent with the DC Circuit’s decision, we have selected a proxy group 
29 
of companies with comparable investment risk to Corning Gas.  In contrast, 
30 
Staff applied less stringent screening criteria, which result in a larger, but 
31 
less comparable proxy group.  
32 
33 
40
Petal Gas Storage v. FERC, 496 F.3d 695, 699-700 (D.C. Cir. 2007).

Q. 
Do you agree with Staff that your proxy group is less comparable to 
2 
Corning Gas than Staff' s proxy group which consists of five gas 
3 
distribution companies and 28 electric utilities? 
4 
A. 
No, we do not. As described in our Direct Testimony, Corning Gas provides 
5 
natural gas distribution service. However, Staffs proxy group of 85 percent 
6 
electric utilities does not appropriately reflect the risks associated with 
7 
Corning Gas' gas distribution operations. While our Combined Utility 
8 
Proxy Group also includes both gas distribution companies and electric 
9 
utilities, the business and operating risks for gas distributors and electric 
10 
utilities are different. As shown in Exhibit 
(AEB/CMW-2R), Staffs 
11 
proxy group does not adequately represent the proportion of the Company's 
12 
business operations that relate to gas distribution. On average, the 
13 
companies in Staff's proxy group derive just 23.00 percent of their regulated 
14 
operating income and 25.00 percent of their regulated operating revenue 
15 
from gas distribution. In contrast, Corning Gas derives 100 percent of its 
16 
regulated operating income and revenue from gas distribution services. In 
17 
fact, as shown in Exhibit 
(AEB/CMW-2R), ten of the companies in 
18 
Staffs proxy group derive no operating revenue or income from natural gas 
19 
operations. In our view, investors would give more weight to market data 
20 
for companies in the gas distribution industry and less weight to electric 
21 
utilities than is implied by Staff's analysis. Thus, Staffs proxy group does 
22 
not adequately reflect the risks of Corning Gas which is exclusively a 
23 
natural gas distribution company.

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Why is net operating income, rather than revenue, the appropriate 
2 
screening criterion to utilize for selecting a proxy group for the 
3 
Company? 
4 
A. 
As discussed extensively in our Direct Testimony,41 net operating income 
5 
better reflects the contribution of each business segment to the corporation's 
6 
earnings. For regulated utilities that collect the cost of purchased gas, fuel, 
7 
and/or power on a pass-through basis through rates, regulated revenue can 
8 
fluctuate materially with changes in weather without affecting the 
9 
corporation's earnings or fmancial position. In contrast, net operating 
10 
income, which excludes purchased commodity costs, more closely 
11 
represents the effect of each business segment on the corporation's overall 
12 
risk profile. 
13 
14 
Q. 
Why does Staff suggest that revenue is the superior screening criterion? 
15 
A. 
Staff argues that using net operating income, rather than revenue, would 
16 
inappropriately exclude comparable companies from the proxy group.' 
17 
Staff further argues that the revenue criterion better accounts for exposure 
18 
to risky competitive operations than the net income screening criterion. 
19 
41

11 
What Staff fails to recognize, however, is revenues that reflect the collection 
12

13

14

15

16

17

18 
screening criteria. However, those fluctuations do not reflect changes in the 
19 
risk profile of the underlying business operation because the changes in the 
20 
cost of goods are simply collected through rates on a pass-through basis.

C. 
APPLICATION OF THE DCF MODEL 
3 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff's application of the DCF model. 
4 
A. 
Staff uses a two-stage DCF model that forecasts dividends from 2020 to 
5 
2024 using Value Line's estimates of projected dividends in that period, and 
6 
a "sustainable growth rate" from 2024 forward. 
Using the DCF 
7 
methodology, Staff calculates a mean ROE for its proxy group of 8.43 
8 
percent. Staff applies a 2/3 weight to its DCF model results in deriving its 
9 
overall ROE recommendation. 
10 
11 
Q. 
What are the differences between your application of the DCF model 
12 
and Staff's approach? 
13 
A. 
The most significant difference between our application of the DCF model 
14 
and Staff's approach is the growth rates we use in our respective analyses. 
15 
We have used a consensus of analysts' EPS growth rates for the proxy group 
16 
companies as the near-term growth rate, and an estimate of growth in the 
17 
overall economy for the long-term growth rate. 
This mitigates the 
18 
uncertainty associated with forecasting individual companies' growth rates 
19 
over very long-time horizons. By contrast, Staff uses dividend growth 
20 
projections from a single source (i.e., Value Line) for their near-term growth 
21 
rate, and a "sustainable growth rate" for their long-term growth rate. Staff 
22 
states that "it is highly unlikely that investors would rely exclusively on the

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1

Q. 
earnings growth rate forecasts of Wall Street analysts in determining short-
term dividend projections".61
Do you agree with Staff's position on earnings growth rates? 
A. 
No, we do not. Staff prefers dividend growth rates rather than earnings 
growth rates as the near-term growth rate in their DCF analysis. However, 
as discussed in our Direct Testimony, dividend growth is fundamentally 
driven by earnings growth.62 
While the model is, indeed, called the 
"Discounted Cash Flow" model, the cash flows it refers to are those an 
investor can expect to receive during the time they own the stock. Those 
cash flows are quarterly dividend payments plus any capital appreciation 
that occurs between when the stock is purchased and when it is sold. 
Dividend payments and capital appreciation are both a function of earnings 
per share ("EPS"), which is ultimately what determines the ROE to the 
investor. As discussed in our Direct Testimony, dividends are based on 
management decisions related to cash management and other factors, and 
therefore dividend growth rates are less likely to accurately reflect 
investors' growth expectations than earnings growth rates.63

1 
We have relied on earnings growth because earnings are the fundamental 
2 
determinant of a company's ability to pay dividends. As noted by Brigham 
3 
and Houston: 
4 
Growth in dividends occurs primarily as a result of 
5 
growth in earnings per share (EPS). Earnings growth, 
6 
in turn, results from a number of factors, including (1) 
7 
inflation, (2) the amount of earnings the company 
8 
retains and invests, and (3) the rate of return the 
9 
company earns on its equity (ROE).64
10 
11 
Investment analysts report predominant reliance on EPS growth 
12 
projections. In a survey completed by 297 members of the Association for 
13 
Investment Management and Research, the majority of respondents ranked 
14 
earnings as the most important variable in valuing a security (more 
15 
important than cash flow, dividends, or book value).65
16 
17 
Academic research also supports the use of EPS growth estimates. A 2002 
18 
study in the Journal of Accounting Research, examined "the valuation 
19 
performance of a comprehensive list of value drivers" and found that 
20 
"forward earnings explain stock prices remarkably well" and were generally 
21 
superior to other value drivers analyzed.66 A 2012 study from the journal 
22 
Contemporary Accounting Research found that the sell-side analysts with

company earns on its equity (ROE).64
9 
10 
Investment analysts report predominant reliance on EPS growth 
11 
projections.  In a survey completed by 297 members of the Association for 
12 
Investment Management and Research, the majority of respondents ranked 
13 
earnings as the most important variable in valuing a security (more 
14 
important than cash flow, dividends, or book value).65
15 
16 
Academic research also supports the use of EPS growth estimates.  A 2002 
17 
study in the Journal of Accounting Research, examined “the valuation 
18 
performance of a comprehensive list of value drivers” and found that 
19 
“forward earnings explain stock prices remarkably well” and were generally 
20 
superior to other value drivers analyzed.66  A 2012 study from the journal 
21 
Contemporary Accounting Research found that the sell-side analysts with 
22

Q. 
According to Staff, the Value Line growth rates are produced by a 
5 
group of analysts, and therefore represent a consensus view. Do you 
6 
agree? 
7 
A. 
No, we do not. Staff provides a letter from Value Line describing the 
8 
development of its growth projections, as Exhibit 
(FP-13). Value Line 
9 
states that each company it covers is assigned to a lead analyst who is 
10 
responsible for building the coverage model for that company. Specifically, 
11 
the letter states, "[e]ach stock in The Value Line Investment Survey is 
12 
assigned to a specific analyst."68 The letter goes on to report the quality 
13 
control procedures applied to the analyst's report. Nowhere in the letter, 
14 
titled "Quality Control Procedures," does Value Line describe a process 
15 
whereby multiple independent evaluations are performed and then averaged 
16 
together to form a consensus view. That is the important element missing 
17 
from Staff's analysis: Staff relies on a source for growth rate estimates that 
18 
reflects the opinion of a single analyst.

20 
While we agree that Value Line is a trusted source for investment 
21 
professionals, it is not the only tool that investors rely on to make decisions.

1 
There are additional data sources readily available that compile the 
2 
consensus viewpoints of multiple brokerage analysts, and it is reasonable to 
3 
expect that investors also consider that information. In fact, there have been 
4 
studies performed comparing Value Line and I/B/E/S analyst earnings 
5 
forecasts in terms of accuracy, rationality and as proxies for market 
6 
expectations. In 2001, a study concluded that "I/B/E/S forecasts were 
7 
superior, as explained by the combination of I/B/E/S's timing advantage 
8 
and the mitigation of idiosyncratic error through consensus building."69
9 
Furthermore, the I/B/E/S long-term forecasts were less biased and more 
10 
accurate.7°

12 
In addition, Staff's position on the appropriate growth rate is contradictory. 
13 
Staff criticizes the use of earnings growth rates because "Rational investors 
14 
will not look at forecasted earnings alone in a vacuum. Rather, they will 
15 
factor in all relevant information when considering an investment."71 If 
16 
investors consider all relevant information, it is inconsistent with that 
17 
principle to limit the DCF analysis to rely exclusively on a single source of 
18 
estimated growth rates, as Staff has done. Based on Staff's conclusion, it is 
19 
reasonable to consider the various sources of consensus growth estimates 
20 
that we have relied on in addition to the Value Line earnings growth rates.

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Is there additional evidence that investors give more weight to earnings 
2 
growth estimates, rather than dividend growth estimates? 
3 
A. 
Yes, there is. As Dr. Roger Morin observed in his treatise, New Regulatory 
4 
Finance, earnings growth forecasts are far more widely available than 
5 
dividend growth forecasts, which indicates that the investment community 
6 
places more weight on earnings growth rates than dividend growth rates. 
7 
Specifically, Dr. Morin stated: 
8 
Casual inspection of the Zacks Investment Research, 
9 
First Call Thompson, and Multex Web sites reveals that 
10 
earnings per share forecasts dominate the information 
11 
provided. 
There are few, if any, dividend growth 
12 
forecasts. Only Value Line provides comprehensive 
13 
long-term dividend growth forecasts. 
The wide 
14 
availability of earnings forecasts is not surprising. 
15 
There is an abundance of evidence attesting to the 
16 
importance of earnings in assessing investors' 
17 
expectations. The sheer volume of earnings forecasts 
18 
available from the investment community relative to the 
19 
scarcity of dividend forecasts attests to their importance. 
20 
The fact that these investment information providers 
21 
focus on growth in earnings rather than growth in 
22 
dividends indicates that the investment community 
23 
regards earnings growth as a superior indicator of future 
24 
long-term growth. Surveys of analytical techniques 
25 
used by analysts reveal the dominance of earnings and 
26 
conclude that earnings are considered far more 
27 
important than dividends. 
Finally, Value Line's 
28 
principle investment rating assigned to individual 
29 
stocks, Timeliness Rank, is based primarily on earnings, 
30 
accounting for 65% of the ranking.72
31 
72 
Roger A Morin, "New Regulatory Finance", Public Utilities Reports, Inc. 2006, at 
302-303.

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Staff observes that the Value Line earnings growth projection for 
2 
Northwest Natural Gas is high relative to the other proxy companies. 
3 
Do you share Staff's concern? 
4 
A. 
No, we do not. While Staff is correct that some companies in our proxy 
5 
group are projected to have different EPS growth rates depending on the 
6 
source considered,73 that is one of the advantages of relying on consensus 
7 
estimates. Relying on multiple sources for the growth rate assumption in 
8 
the DCF model mitigates the effect of outlier growth rates. In contrast, 
9 
Staffs approach, which places exclusive reliance on a single source, is more 
10 
susceptible to the effect of outlier growth rates. For example, Staff is 
11 
relying on an a projected DPS growth rate of approximately 0.86 percent 
12 
for NWN during the first stage of Staffs multi-stage DCF model. This 
13 
growth rate is unreasonably low and could also be considered an outlier. 
14 
15 
Q. 
Do you agree with Staff that reliance on Value Line growth rates lends 
16 
a degree of predictability to the New York rate setting process for the 
17 
investment community? 
18 
A. 
No, we do not. The investment community reviews the returns established 
19 
through the New York rate setting process based on the reasonableness and 
20 
comparability of the returns to other investment alternatives. 
21 
73

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Please summarize your concerns with Staff's sustainable growth rate. 
2 
A. 
We have two main concerns with Staff's sustainable growth rate. First, 
3 
while Staff asserts that its calculation of the sustainable growth rate 
4 
represents a measure of long-term growth for the period 2025 and beyond, 
5 
the inputs to its calculation are, for the most part, shorter-term estimates for 
6 
the period 2024 and earlier. For example, the "b * r" component of Staff's 
7 
sustainable growth rate is derived from Value Line forecasts that only 
8 
extend through 2024. Therefore, Staff's long-term growth rate estimate 
9 
reflects, at best, one analyst's forecast of only the very early years of the 
10 
second stage of Staff's DCF model, which theoretically extends into 
11 
perpetuity. In contrast, our estimate of long-term GDP growth reflects 
12 
projections over 30 years or more and considers overall measures of 
13 
economic growth.74 Second, Staff's sustainable growth rate relies on Value 
14 
Line's estimate of each proxy company's ROE, as the "r" in the "b * r" 
15 
component of Staff's growth rate is the expected ROE. This introduces an 
16 
element of circularity into Staff's calculation, that it fails to reconcile with 
17 
its results. That is, based on Exhibit 
(FP-6), the mean and median ROEs 
18 
assumed in Staff's DCF calculation are 10.73 percent and 10.55 percent, 
19 
respectively, yet Staff's analysis suggests the proxy group average ROE is 
20 
8.45 percent. By that measure, the range of ROEs established in our 
21 
analysis for our proxy group companies (L e., 9.50 percent to 10.50 percent) 
74 
Direct Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall, at 60-63.

2

3 
represents a measure of long-term growth for the period 2025 and beyond, 
4

5 
the period 2024 and earlier.  For example, the “b * r” component of Staff’s 
6 
sustainable growth rate is derived from Value Line forecasts that only 
7 
extend through 2024.  Therefore, Staff’s long-term growth rate estimate 
8 
reflects, at best, one analyst’s forecast of only the very early years of the 
9 
second stage of Staff’s DCF model, which theoretically extends into 
10 
perpetuity.  In contrast, our estimate of long-term GDP growth reflects 
11 
projections over 30 years or more and considers overall measures of 
12 
economic growth.74  Second, Staff’s sustainable growth rate relies on Value 
13 
Line’s estimate of each proxy company’s ROE, as the “r” in the “b * r” 
14 
component of Staff’s growth rate is the expected ROE.  This introduces an 
15 
element of circularity into Staff’s calculation, that it fails to reconcile with 
16 
its results.  That is, based on Exhibit __ (FP-6), the mean and median ROEs 
17 
assumed in Staff’s DCF calculation are 10.73 percent and 10.55 percent, 
18 
respectively, yet Staff’s analysis suggests the proxy group average ROE is 
19 
8.45 percent.  By that measure, the range of ROEs established in our 
20 
analysis for our proxy group companies (i.e., 9.50 percent to 10.50 percent)

74
Direct Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall, at 60-63.

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
is quite conservative relative to the proxy group ROEs assumed in Staff's 
2 
sustainable growth estimate.75
3 
4 
Q. 
Is there academic research that recognizes the shortcomings of the 
5 
sustainable growth rate approach? 
6 
A. 
Yes. Dr. Roger Morin noted his concern with the use of the sustainable 
7 
growth rates in the DCF. Specifically, Dr. Morin stated: 
8 
In summary, there are three proxies for the expected 
9 
growth component of the DCF model: historical growth 
10 
rates, analysts' forecasts, and the sustainable growth 
11 
method. Criteria in choosing among the three proxies 
12 
should include ease of use, ease of understanding, 
13 
theoretical and mathematical correctness, and empirical 
14 
validation. The latter two are crucial. The method 
15 
should be logically valid and consistent, and should 
16 
possess an adequate track record in predicting and 
17 
explaining security value. The retention growth method 
18 
is the weakest of the three proxies on both conceptual 
19 
and empirical grounds. The research in this area has 
20 
shown that the first two growth proxies do a better job 
21 
of explaining variations in market valuations (M/B and 
22 
P/E ratios) and are more highly correlated to measures 
23 
of value than is the retention growth proxy.76
24 
25 
In light of the theoretical and empirical concerns noted, it is reasonable to 
26 
conclude, as we have, that the sustainable growth rate should not be relied 
27 
on as the estimate of the long-term growth rate in the multi-stage DCF 
28 
model. 
29 
75 
Id., at 96. 
76 
Roger A Morin, "New Regulatory Finance", Public Utilities Reports, Inc. 2006, at 
307.

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Do the GDP growth projections referenced by Staff as a check on the 
2 
reasonableness of Staff's sustainable growth rate account for the effect 
3 
of the business cycle? 
4 
A. 
No, they do not. Specifically, Staff references a real GDP growth projection 
5 
from the 2020 Annual Energy Outlook published by EIA from 2020 through 
6 
2050. However, EIA notes explicitly that "all three economic growth cases 
7 
assume smooth economic growth and do not anticipate business cycles or 
8 
large economic shocks."77 Thus, ETA's projection of real GDP growth will 
9 
most likely understate the long-term growth in real GDP since the 
10 
methodology does not consider business cycles and relies on current trends 
11 
in economic data. This is a particularly meaningful shortcoming in light of 
12 
the recent economic downturn associated with the current health pandemic. 
13 
Therefore, ETA's projections are not a relevant check on the reasonableness 
14 
of the GDP growth rate reflected in our multi-stage DCF analysis or the 
15 
sustainable growth rate relied on in Staff's multi-state DCF analysis. 
16 
17 
Q. 
Has Staff relied on historical data in any of their analyses? 
18 
A. 
Yes. For example, in its CAPM analysis, Staff relies exclusively on Value 
19 
Line Beta coefficients that use a 5-year historical estimate of the relative 
20 
risk of proxy companies and the overall market. 
21 
77

(FP-10), at 10.

--- Page 61 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Does Staff's justification for relying on Value Line's estimated Beta 
2 
coefficients also support your reliance on historical GDP growth in the 
3 
DCF model? 
4 
A. 
Yes, it does. As discussed in more detail in Section W, part D of our 
5 
Rebuttal Testimony, Staff notes that Value Line's Beta coefficients were 
6 
selected for use in the CAPM because the Beta coefficients are calculated 
7 
using a relatively longer historical timeframe, which moderates the effect 
8 
of short-term volatility in the market on the estimate of the Beta coefficients. 
9 
This logic is consistent with our use of the historical growth rate in real GDP 
10 
to estimate the long-term growth rate in our DCF analysis. The economic 
11 
business cycle has historically been very difficult to predict. Thus, a 
12 
projected real GDP growth rate at a given point in time may either under or 
13 
overestimate projected real GDP depending on what stage of the business 
14 
cycle the economy is in at that time of the projection. As a result, it is more 
15 
appropriate to use a long-term historical estimate of real GDP growth which 
16 
takes into account the effect of multiple business cycles over the course of 
17 
time and better approximates the long-term average growth in real GDP. 
18 
19 
Q. 
Do you have any additional concerns with the projected GDP growth 
20 
rate cited by Staff? 
21 
A. 
Yes. The GDP growth projections cited by Staff cover relatively short time 
22 
periods. For example, Staff cites to the ETA's base case real GDP growth

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
Page 59 of 122
Q.
Does Staff’s justification for relying on Value Line’s estimated Beta 
1 
coefficients also support your reliance on historical GDP growth in the 
2 
DCF model? 
3 
A.
Yes, it does.  As discussed in more detail in Section IV, part D of our 
4 
Rebuttal Testimony, Staff notes that Value Line’s Beta coefficients were 
5 
selected for use in the CAPM because the Beta coefficients are calculated 
6 
using a relatively longer historical timeframe, which moderates the effect 
7 
of short-term volatility in the market on the estimate of the Beta coefficients.  
8 
This logic is consistent with our use of the historical growth rate in real GDP 
9 
to estimate the long-term growth rate in our DCF analysis.  The economic 
10 
business cycle has historically been very difficult to predict. Thus, a 
11 
projected real GDP growth rate at a given point in time may either under or 
12 
overestimate projected real GDP depending on what stage of the business 
13 
cycle the economy is in at that time of the projection.  As a result, it is more 
14 
appropriate to use a long-term historical estimate of real GDP growth which 
15 
takes into account the effect of multiple business cycles over the course of 
16 
time and better approximates the long-term average growth in real GDP. 
17 
18

rate cited by Staff?

21 
periods.  For example, Staff cites to the EIA’s base case real GDP  growth 
22 
projection of 1.90 percent for the period 2020 through 2050 as evidence that

Q. 
How do you respond? 
2 
A. 
Every utility company in the proxy group pays dividends on a quarterly 
3 
basis. The timing reflected in our multi-stage DCF model reflects the 
4 
assumption that dividends are paid, on average, halfway through the year 
5 
(i.e., two dividends are paid in the first half of the year, and two dividends 
6 
are paid in the second half of the year). To assume, as Staff suggests, that 
7 
dividends are not paid until the end of the year would not properly reflect 
8 
the quarterly nature of the proxy group's dividend payments. Staff s 
9 
suggested approach would result in the DCF model understating investor's 
10 
return requirements. 
11 
12 
Q.

13 
current market conditions? 
14

15

16 
market conditions as a result of unusual levels of market volatility and high 
17 
utility stock valuations. High valuations on utility shares, as noted by 
18 
multiple equity analysts, could result in an underestimation of the cost of 
19 
equity using the DCF model, especially if those high valuations are not 
20 
sustainable in the future. For these reasons, we believe it is appropriate to 
21 
afford an appropriate weight to the results of other ROE estimation 
22 
methodologies. In particular, the CAPM is more sensitive to changes

D. 
APPLICATION OF THE CAPM 
5 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff's application of the CAPM. 
6 
A. 
Staff developed its estimate of the risk-free rate of 1.05 percent by using a 
7 
recent average of 10-year and 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond yields. For the 
8 
Beta estimate, Staff used the average Value Line Beta for its proxy group 
9 
of 0.74. To estimate the market risk premium, Staff subtracted its risk-free 
10 
rate estimate from an average of the forecast returns for the S&P 500 from 
11 
Merrill Lynch's March, April and May 2020 Quantitative Profiles. Staff 
12 
also developed a zero-Beta CAPM using similar inputs as its traditional 
13 
CAPM analysis but using a market risk premium based on weighting the 
14 
Beta times the market risk premium by 0.75 and the market risk premium 
15 
itself by 0.25. Staff relied on the average of its two CAPM analyses and 
16 
weighted that result by one-third in the formulation of its overall ROE 
17 
recommendation. 
18 
19 
Q. 
What are the primary differences between your application of the 
20 
CAPM and Staff's application of this model? 
21 
A. 
Because the estimation of the ROE is a forward-looking concept, and the 
22 
ROE that is authorized in these cases will be in effect for some period in the 
23 
future, our analysis appropriately considers both the recent historical risk-
24 
free rate and the projected risk-free rate. The analysis presented in our

Staffs rationale for relying on 10-year and 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond 
10 
yields in its CAPM analysis is that it is consistent with the approach the 
11 
Commission has relied on in prior cases, and the use of these securities 
12 
reflects the expectations of investors who have "both intermediate and long-
13 
term investment horizons.89 We disagree with Staff's rationale. As noted 
14 
by Morningstar, "the horizon is a function of the investment, not the 
15 
investor." The use of the 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond yield as the risk-free 
16 
rate is consistent with the investment horizon for natural gas utility assets. 
17 
Taking Staff's rationale to the limits, an investor who plans to hold a 
18 
position in a utility equity share for only six months would use a certificate 
19 
of deposit rate to evaluate the potential return. That is clearly not the case 
20 
for any rational investor considering return requirements.

Q. 
Why are projected Treasury Bond yields informative in the CAPM 
2

3

4

5

6

7

8

9

10

12

13

14

18

Q. 
Please summarize Staff's concern with your use of Bloomberg's Beta 
2 
coefficients in the CAPM analysis. 
3 
A. 
Staff contends that the Commission has consistently preferred Value Line's 
4 
Beta coefficients because they are more accurate, and they reflect the 
5 
exercise of expert judgment. To support this position, Staff cites a study 
6 
analyzing the relative accuracy of Value Line's Beta coefficients compared 
7 
to other sources.95
8 
9 
Q. 
Why is it reasonable to rely on Bloomberg's Beta coefficients? 
10 
A. 
It is reasonable to consider several measures of market conditions in 
11 
estimating the ROE. As noted in our Direct Testimony, the Bloomberg Beta 
12 
coefficient is widely used, and because it is based on a ten-year period as 
13 
compared to Value Line's five-year period, it mitigates the exogenous effect 
14 
of the TCJA on utility Beta coefficients.96 The academic study cited by 
15 
Staff was published before the Senate passed the TCJA on December 2, 
16 
2017. As illustrated by Figure 10 in our Direct Testimony, the performance 
17 
of utility stocks deviated substantially from the performance of the broader 
18 
market following the passage of the TCJA.

Q. 
Does Staff's reasoning for selecting the five-year Beta coefficients from 
2 
Value Line also support the use of ten-year Beta coefficients from 
3 
Bloomberg? 
4 
A.

5 
Value Line Beta coefficients: 
6 
Another, and perhaps more significant, shortcoming of 
7 
beta calculations is the often wide disparity of betas 
8 
between the various firms that report this measure. For 
9 
instance, Staff has exclusively relied on betas reported 
10 
by Value Line for at least the past twenty years, as they 
11 
are generally calculated over a five-year period. This is 
12 
long enough to produce reliable estimates but recent 
13 
enough to reflect current market conditions. Some firms 
14 
use either much shorter or much longer time periods in 
15 
their calculations of beta. We find that the five-year 
16 
time period used by Value Line produces reliable 
17 
estimates of volatility.97
18 
19 
Staff recognizes that dislocations in the market can skew Beta coefficient 
20 
estimates; therefore, it is appropriate to select a longer-time period to 
21 
mitigate the effect of short-term aberrations in the market. Although, Staff 
22 
has failed to consider that some short-term aberrations in the market can be 
23 
large enough that using a five-year period may not be sufficient enough to 
24 
mitigate the effect of the short-term aberration on the calculation of Beta 
25 
coefficients. This is demonstrated by the Value Line Beta coefficients 
26 
reported by Staff in this case. As Staff notes, Value Line's Beta coefficients 
27 
have increased markedly over the last few months.98 This is likely due to 
28 
COVID-19 which caused a short-term aberration in the market. Thus, given

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff's concern with your estimation of the Market 
2 
Risk Premium. 
3 
A. 
Staff testifies that the use of the Constant Growth DCF model to develop 
4 
the market return on the S&P 500 is not appropriate because the companies 
5 
in the S&P 500 cannot sustain their three-to-five year growth rates in 
6 
perpetuity.101 
7 
8 
Q. 
What is your response? 
9 
A. 
The calculation of the MRP is based on the return on the broader market, as 
10 
measured by S&P 500, less the return on a risk-free instrument. The S&P 
11 
500 is an index that includes the largest 500 companies by market 
12 
capitalization. Over time, the specific companies that are included in the 
13 
S&P 500 Index will vary, but investor expectations of growth and return 
14 
overall for the index as a whole may not, based on the selection process 
15 
involved in the index. Therefore, it is reasonable to assume that the average 
16 
growth of the index could be sustainable in the long run. 
17 
18 
Q. 
Is there additional support for the reasonableness of the market return 
19 
you have used to calculate the forward-looking market risk premium? 
20 
A. 
Yes, other alternative sources provide reputable forecasts of market returns 
21 
that are significantly higher than the estimated market return relied on by 
22 
Staff. For example, S&P regularly publishes earnings growth projections

measured by S&P 500, less the return on a risk-free instrument.  The S&P 
10 
500 is an index that includes the largest 500 companies by market 
11 
capitalization.  Over time, the specific companies that are included in the 
12 
S&P 500 Index will vary, but investor expectations of growth and return 
13 
overall for the index as a whole may not, based on the selection process 
14 
involved in the index.  Therefore, it is reasonable to assume that the average 
15 
growth of the index could be sustainable in the long run. 
16 
17

18 
you have used to calculate the forward-looking market risk premium? 
19 
A.

20 
that are significantly higher than the estimated market return relied on by 
21 
Staff.  For example, S&P regularly publishes earnings growth projections 
22

--- Page 83 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Please elaborate on your concerns. 
2 
A. 
Staff has provided credit analyses to justify its recommended ROE and 
3 
equity ratio on the basis that its recommendation would allow Corning Gas 
4 
to maintain financial metrics that would be considered investment grade 
5 
according to Moody's and S&P. This approach to estimating the investor-
6 
required ROE is inappropriate. The standards for a just and reasonable 
7 
return outlined in Hope and Bluefield were not that the return for a company 
8 
be set at a level such that the company's resulting financial metrics be set at 
9 
what credit rating agencies would classify as investment grade. 
The 
10 
standards outlined in Hope and Bluefield were that the return be set at a level 
11 
that is adequate to attract capital at reasonable terms and consistent with 
12 
returns on equity investments in companies having comparable risk. Staff's 
13 
credit metrics analysis has failed to demonstrate that its recommended ROE 
14 
and equity ratio are consistent with the returns on assets with similar risk. 
15 
As shown in Exhibit 
(AEB/CMW_3R), Staff's recommended ROE is 
16 
more than 100-basis points lower than the average ROE authorized for the 
17 
operating subsidiaries of our Combined Utility Proxy Group. Additionally, 
18 
Staffs recommended equity ratio is more than 500-basis points lower than 
19 
the average authorized equity ratio for the operating subsidiaries of our 
20 
Combined Utility Proxy Group. Further, as discussed in Section IV,F 
21 
below, Corning Gas faces a higher level of business and financial risk than 
22 
the companies in our Combined Utility Proxy Group. 
23

REGULATORY FRAMEWORK AND BUSINESS RISKS 
2 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff's position regarding the Company's business 
3 
risks. 
4 
A.

5 
segment as a whole to support conclusions regarding the risk of Corning 
6 
Gas. Staff's review of the Company's business risk is as compared to the 
7 
overall industry risk for utilities and does not provide any evidence as to the 
8 
relative risk of Corning Gas and the proxy group.123 Because the ROE is 
9 
estimated using the proxy group results as a surrogate for the investor 
10 
required return, it is important that any analysis of the credit supportiveness 
11 
and business risk of the Company be conducted relative to the proxy group. 
12 
Staff further contends that the business risk analyses summarized in our 
13 
Direct Testimony, which appropriately compared the relative risk of 
14 
Corning Gas and the proxy group, is "simplistic" because it did not account 
15 
for the relative size of the operating subsidiaries of the proxy group 
16 
companies. Finally, Staff rejects our proposed small size adjustment of 50 
17 
basis points for Corning Gas.

the Size Effect: An Empirical Analysis" to suggest that investors do not

Q. 
Please summarize the academic research that Staff consulted regarding 
5 
the size premium for regulated utilities. 
6 
A. 
The Wong study cited by Staff analyzed the effect of firm size on monthly, 
7 
weekly, and daily estimates of the Beta coefficients for a sample of utility 
8 
companies and a sample of industrial companies over four sub-periods 
9 
beginning in 1968 and ending in 1987.130 Wong concludes that utility Beta 
10 
coefficients do not decrease as firm size increases. Wong attributes this 
11 
fmding to the possibility that utility regulation reduces risk regardless of 
12 
fn-m size.131
13 
14 
Q. 
How do you respond to Wong's analysis? 
15 
A. 
There are multiple reasons why a smaller company may not always have 
16 
the highest Beta coefficient. For example, smaller companies are traded 
17 
less frequently than larger companies. A lower trading frequency can bias 
18 
the estimate of the Beta coefficient. Thomas Zepp specifically identifies 
19 
this as an issue in Wong's analysis in his article "Utility Stocks and the Size 
20 
Effect — Revisited":

1 
Being unable to demonstrate a relationship between size 
2 
and beta risk for utilities in other periods may be the 
3 
result of Wong using monthly, weekly, and daily data to 
4 
make those beta estimates. 
Roll (1980) concluded 
5 
trading infrequency seems to be a very powerful cause 
6 
of bias in beta risk estimates when time intervals of a 
7 
month or less are used to estimate betas for small stocks. 
8 
When a small stock is thinly traded, its stock price does 
9 
not reflect the movement of the market, which drives 
10 
down the apparent covariance with the market and 
11 
creates an artificially low beta estimate. 132
12 
13 
Zepp's observation is consistent with Staffs testimony in this case: 
14 
Often sales of stocks that are thinly traded will cause 
15 
market prices to change abruptly, especially if a large 
16 
block of shares is sold at once. When such a sale occurs, 
17 
the sale price may be far removed from the stock's true 
18 
value. Therefore, at any point in time, the sale price may 
19 
not match the true value of the stock, and a direct cost 
20 
of equity analysis would not yield accurate results.133
21 
22 
In fact, Zepp showed that Beta coefficients for a sample of water companies 
23 
were greater when annual data (i.e., the approach employed by Ibbotson 
24 
Associates in a study cited in our Direct Testimony134) was used to estimate 
25 
the Beta coefficient than the Beta coefficients reported by Value Line which 
26 
use weekly data.1"

--- Page 98 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Is there additional evidence that Beta coefficients for small companies 
2 
do not account for all the risk associated with small-sized firms? 
3 
A. 
Yes, there is. For example, Figure 8 contains the average Beta coefficient, 
4 
average arithmetic annual return and average annual standard deviation for 
5 
the companies included in each size decile developed by Duff and Phelps. 
6 
As shown in Figure 8, the average annual arithmetic return for the tenth 
7 
decile (i.e., the decile in which Corning Gas would be classified) was 19.87 
8 
percent. This equates to an equity risk premium of 14.93 percent if the long-
9 
term income only return of 4.94 percent from long-term government bonds 
10 
is subtracted from the total annual return. Conversely, we could also 
11 
estimate the equity risk premium using the Beta coefficient for the tenth 
12 
decile and the historical market risk premium as reported by Duff and 
13 
Phelps from 1926-2019. If we multiply the historical market risk premium 
14 
as reported by Duff and Phelps of 7.15 percent136 by the Beta coefficient for 
15 
the tenth decile of 1.39, the resulting equity risk premium is 9.94 percent. 
16 
Thus, calculating the equity risk premium using the Beta coefficient 
17 
significantly understates the actual long-term equity risk premium for 
18 
companies in the tenth decile. Therefore, the Beta coefficient does not 
19 
appropriately account for the additional risk associated with small size. 
136 
The market risk premium from 1926-2019 is calculated as the average return on 
large company stocks from 1926-2019 minus the average income only return on 
long-term government bonds from 1926-2019 (i.e., 12.09 percent — 4.94 percent = 
7.15 percent). Source: Duff &Phelps, Cost of Capital Navigator, CRSP Deciles 
Size Study — Supplementary Data Exhibits, p. 2.

--- Page 100 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
Is the size premium applicable to companies in regulated industries 
2 
such as natural gas utilities? 
3 
A. 
Yes, it is. In fact, Stephanie Chretien and Frank Coggins in the article "Cost 
4 
of Equity for Energy Utilities: Beyond the CAPM", recently studied the 
5 
CAPM and its ability to estimate the risk premium for the utility industry in 
6 
particular subgroups of utilities. One of the subgroups was a group of 
7 
natural gas distribution companies that contained many of the same natural 
8 
gas distribution companies included in our proxy group.139 The article 
9 
considered the CAPM, the Fama-French three-factor model and a model 
10 
similar to the Empirical CAPM that we have also considered above. In the 
11 
article, the Fama-French three-factor model explicitly included an 
12 
adjustment to the CAPM for risk associated with size. As Chretien and 
13 
Coggins show, the Beta coefficient on the size variable for the U.S. natural 
14 
gas utility group was positive and statistically significant indicating that 
15 
small size risk was relevant for regulated natural gas utilities.140 This 
16 
demonstrates that the traditional CAPM model would not account for risk 
17 
associated with small size. 
18 
139 
The U.S. natural gas utility group included: AGL Resources Inc., Atmos Energy 
Corp., Laclede Group, New Jersey Resources Corp., Northwest Natural Gas Co., 
Piedmont Natural Gas Co., South Jersey Industries, Southwest Gas Corp. and 
WGL Holdings Inc. 
140 
Chretien, Stephane, and Frank Coggins. "Cost Of Equity For Energy Utilities: 
Beyond The CAPM." Energy Studies Review, vol. 18, no. 2, 2011, 
doi:10.15173/esr.v18i2.531, at 31. 
Page 98 of 122 
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
Page 98 of 122
Q.
Is the size premium applicable to companies in regulated industries 
1 
such as natural gas utilities? 
2 
A.
Yes, it is.  In fact, Stephanie Chretien and Frank Coggins in the article “Cost 
3 
of Equity for Energy Utilities: Beyond the CAPM”, recently studied the 
4 
CAPM and its ability to estimate the risk premium for the utility industry in 
5 
particular subgroups of utilities. One of the subgroups was a group of 
6 
natural gas distribution companies that contained many of the same natural 
7 
gas distribution companies included in our proxy group.139  The article 
8 
considered the CAPM, the Fama-French three-factor model and a model 
9 
similar to the Empirical CAPM that we have also considered above.  In the 
10 
article, the Fama-French three-factor model explicitly included an 
11 
adjustment to the CAPM for risk associated with size.  As Chretien and 
12 
Coggins show, the Beta coefficient on the size variable for the U.S. natural 
13 
gas utility group was positive and statistically significant indicating that 
14 
small size risk was relevant for regulated natural gas utilities.140  This 
15 
demonstrates that the traditional CAPM model would not account for risk 
16 
associated with small size.    
17 
18 
139
The U.S. natural gas utility group included: AGL Resources Inc., Atmos Energy 
Corp., Laclede Group, New Jersey Resources Corp., Northwest Natural Gas Co., 
Piedmont Natural Gas Co., South Jersey Industries, Southwest Gas Corp. and 
WGL Holdings Inc. 
140
Chrétien, Stéphane, and Frank Coggins. “Cost Of Equity For Energy Utilities: 
Beyond The CAPM.” Energy Studies Review, vol. 18, no. 2, 2011, 
doi:10.15173/esr.v18i2.531, at 31.

Q. 
How does the market capitalization of Staff's proxy group compare in 
2 
size to Corning Gas? 
3 
A.

4 
than even the smallest company in Staff's proxy group. Similar to the 
5 
approach we used in our Direct Testimony, we estimated the size of the 
6 
Company's market capitalization relative Staff's proxy group by applying 
7 
the Company's proposed capital structure equity component of $35.8 
8 
million to the median market-to-book ratio for Staff' proxy group of 1.93. 
9 
This resulted in an implied market capitalization of approximately $69.0 
10 
million. In contrast, the smallest company by market capitalization in 
11 
Staff's proxy group is Northwest Natural Gas Company, which has a 30-
12 
day average market capitalization of $1.83 billion as of June 30, 2020.141 In 
13 
other words, Coming Gas is less than 4.00 percent the size of the smallest 
14 
company in Staff's proxy group. The largest company in Staff's proxy 
15 
group, NextEra Energy, Inc. (a company that is excluded from our 
16 
Combined Utility Proxy Group), is more than 1,700 times larger than 
17 
Corning Gas.

Q.
What are your conclusions regarding the effect of the Company’s small 
19

20 
A.
We continue to believe that a small size adjustment is appropriate for 
21 
Corning Gas.  As described in our Direct Testimony, smaller utility 
22 
141
Source: Bloomberg Professional. 


--- Page 102 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
companies are less able than larger utility companies to withstand adverse 
2 
events such as the reduction of demand due to macroeconomic conditions 
3 
(such as is happening now due to the pandemic-related economic downturn) 
4 
because they have smaller customer bases, more limited financial resources, 
5 
and less diversification.142 Corning Gas is no exception. Other regulators, 
6 
such as those in Alaska and Minnesota, have recognized the effect of size 
7 
on utility companies' risk.143 The academic research that Staff cites from 
8 
the 1990s regarding the effect of size on utility Beta coefficients is of limited 
9 
relevance because, as Staff themselves note, the stock of smaller companies 
10 
tends to be less liquid. The less liquid a stock, the higher likelihood the 
11 
estimate of the Beta coefficient will be biased if more granular time 
12 
intervals are used in the estimation process. Therefore, the study conducted 
13 
by Wong inappropriately concluded that a size premium adjustment was not 
14 
needed for the utility industry. However, there have been numerous studies 
15 
that have provided support for the use of a size premium adjustment to 
16 
account for the additional risk associated with small size in the regulated 
17 
utility sector. Therefore, it is reasonable to adjust the results of the 
18 
traditional cost of equity models by 50 basis points to reflect the incremental 
19 
risks that Corning Gas faces due to its small size.

4

13

14

15 
to cost recovery of its existing investments. The Commission's plans to 
16 
meet the CLCPA requirements include the electrification of transportation 
17 
and heating sectors, both of which will require significant investment and 
18

19 
service providing service to customers. Investors also understand that for 
20 
Corning Gas, which does not provide electric service, this program would 
21 
likely lead to an ongoing decline in the number of customers over time.

Q. 
Do you agree with Staff as to the appropriate use of a hypothetical 
8 
capital structure? 
9 
A. 
No, we do not. Staff states that a hypothetical capital structure should be 
10 
used "when neither the utility's nor its parent company's actual capital 
11 
structures clearly reflect the reasonable financing policies that would result 
12 
in the lowest cost of capital."' There is no case law of which we are aware 
13 
that suggests the goal in setting the cost of capital is the lowest long-run 
14 
cost of capital. Rather, the landmark cases Hope and Bluefield establish that 
15 
the return to shareholders must be commensurate with the returns available 
16 
on other investments of comparable risk. Staff's recommendations in this 
17 
case establish an equity cost rate that is not commensurate with the risks of 
18 
Corning Gas, or of any of the New York utilities for which the cost of capital 
19 
has been recently established.

1 
Q. 
Do you agree with Staff that it is appropriate to analyze the common 
2 
equity ratios of utility holding companies, rather than the common 
3 
equity ratios of the operating subsidiaries? 
4 
A. 
No, we do not. The capital structure of a utility holding company is based 
5 
on the financing of all the regulated and non-regulated operating 
6 
subsidiaries of that holding company and is not specific to the operating 
7 
subsidiary's operations. As such, Staff's analysis of holding company 
8 
capital structures includes corporate-level debt that is not part of the 
9 
regulated or fmancial capital structure of the operating utilities. The 
10 
relevant capital structure for comparison purposes is at the operating 
11 
company level, not the holding company level. The Commission in this 
12 
case will be setting the capital structure for Corning Gas, the operating 
13 
company, which will be used to finance investments in rate base that 
14 
provide natural gas distribution services to customers.

7

14 
the equity ratio of utility operating subsidiaries.164

1 
mitigate the cash flow pressures created by external events, the Commission 
2 
has not. Moody's notes that this offsets what is otherwise a supportive and 
3 
predictable regulatory environment, stating: 
4 
The New York regulatory framework is one of the most 
5 
predictable and transparent US state energy regulators, 
6 
in Moody's view, with a track record of providing a 
7 
credit-supportive suite of cost recovery mechanisms. 
8 
However, the corollary of this is that the NY PSC have 
9 
tended to (1) offer a lower than average return on equity 
10 
levels (both equity thickness and allowed RoE); and (2) 
11 
follow a more mechanistic approach to setting these 
12 
parameters even when external pressures on operational 
13 
cash flows have arisen. US tax reform has depressed 
14 
operational cash flows for all US utilities, by around 
15 
150-250 basis points on cash flow from operations pre-
16 
working capital (CFO pre-WC) to debt metrics. Some 
17 
state regulators have offset the adverse impact on 
18 
regulated utilities credit quality by increasing the equity 
19 
layer in the regulatory capital structure and/or not 
20 
reflecting the full fall in yields from the sustained low 
21 
yield environment in allowed RoEs. By contrast, the 
22 
NYPSC has, to-date, made limited concessions.165
23 
24 
While Moody's does acknowledge that the New York regulatory

Q. 
Has the Commission previously accepted the use of a higher common 
2 
equity ratio to mitigate the effect of external events on a utility's cash 
3 
flow? 
4 
A. 
Yes. In Central Hudson Gas & Electric's ("Central Hudson") recent rate 
5 
proceeding, the Commission authorized an equity ratio for Central Hudson 
6 
of 48.00 percent in Rate Year 1, 49.00 percent in Rate Year 2, and 50.00 
7 
percent in Rate Year 3.168 Specifically, the Commission noted: 
8 
Given the degree of uncertainty regarding the ultimate 
9 
impact 
of the 
Tax 
Act 
on 
the 
Company's 
10 
creditworthiness, we find the JP's use of higher 
11 
common equity ratios in Rate Year 2 and Rate Year 3 to 
12 
be a responsible and reasonable measure to forestall, or 
13 
at least diminish, the prospect of higher future 
14 
borrowing costs attributable to a diminution in Central 
15 
Hudson's creditworthiness over the next several 
16 
years.169
17 
18 
The pressures on the Company's cash flow metrics are now more 
19 
pronounced as the Company is experiencing pressure created by the TCJA 
20 
and more recently the economic effects of COVID-19. Accordingly, a 
21 
higher common equity ratio for Corning Gas is appropriate in order to 
22 
minimize the effect of those cash flow pressures on the Company's future 
23 
borrowing costs. 
24

Rebuttal Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley and Christopher M. Wall 
1 
Q. 
What do you conclude regarding the appropriate ratemaking equity 
2 
ratio for the Company? 
3 
A. 
We reach five conclusions about the appropriate equity ratio for the 
4 
Company: (1) Corning Gas is sufficiently ring-fenced from its affiliates, 
5 
therefore it is appropriate to rely on Corning Gas' stand-alone equity ratio 
6 
for ratemaking purposes according to Staffs own methodology, (2) Staff's 
7 
recommended equity ratio, combined with its recommended return on 
8 
equity, would result in a weighted equity cost rate for the Company that is 
9 
unreasonably low relative to other recently authorized weighted equity cost 
10 
rates in New York, (3) Corning Gas' requested common equity ratio is 
11 
conservative as compared to the operating subsidiaries in our Combined 
12 
Utility Proxy Group (i.e., utilities with risk profiles that are similar to the 
13 
Company's risk profile), which have common equity ratios that are, on 
14 
average, materially higher than 50.77 percent; (4) Corning Gas' requested 
15 
common equity ratio is well within the range of common equity ratios 
16 
authorized for the operating subsidiaries in our Combined Utility Proxy 
17 
Group; and (5) as the Commission has previously recognized, it is 
18 
reasonable to rely on a higher equity ratio than Corning Gas may have relied 
19 
on in prior cases due to the cash flow concerns raised by credit rating 
20 
agencies as a result of both the coronavirus pandemic and the TCJA. 
21 
Therefore, we continue to support the Company's proposed 50.77 percent 
22 
common equity ratio. 
23

Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding the appropriate capital structure 
9 
for the Company? 
10 
A.

11 
Gas is inconsistent with the financing practices employed by the natural gas 
12 
distribution utility industry. Therefore, we conclude that the appropriate 
13 
capital structure for Corning Gas is the stand-alone capital structure with a 
14 
50.77 percent common equity ratio.
